TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The news about Bangladesh’s measles crisis —already the world’s most severe—keeps getting worse. On Monday, an independent committee of national and international experts submitted a report to the country’s government declaring the outbreak a “litmus test and a warning sign of a deteriorating immunization system.” The report warns that without urgent steps, Bangladesh could experience major outbreaks of other serious diseases, including pertussis, or whooping cough; diphtheria; and even wild polio, which remains endemic in only two countries, Afghanistan and Pakistan. The experts’ warnings come on the heels of Reuters reporting last week based in part on new World Health Organization data. According to Reuters, there were 202,000 suspected measles cases in Bangladesh from mid-March through Oct. 9, leading to 1,147 deaths—94 percent of them among children under 5. The latest government figures , published this week, indicate that more than 1,200 children have now died in the outbreak since March. Six children died in a 24-hour period from Sunday to Monday—raising further concerns that the death rate could rise if the crisis isn’t properly addressed. Experts say the outbreak’s origins trace back to political instability in 2024, when mass protests ousted Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina. The turmoil that followed interrupted vaccination campaigns, and the country hasn’t gotten back on track. The crisis is compounded by poor data gathering, resulting in millions of children missing vaccines. In its haste to move quickly on new inoculation efforts, the government that assumed power this year hasn’t implemented enough readiness checks prior to its vaccination campaigns, causing additional complications.   Foreign Policy | 28 Oct 19:20 UTC
  Main opposition parties fail to muster majority.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Here’s one way to think about the confusing results of Israel’s general election on Tuesday: Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu didn’t win, but he is in the driver’s seat by virtue of the fact that everybody else lost—not just his opponents but his allies, each in their own way. The result is that Netanyahu has no easy path to forming a government and virtually no chance at all of reestablishing the religious far-right coalition that he has led for the past four years. But he could end up at the head of a center-right government with some of the opposition parties. And, failing that, he will lead a caretaker government that will leave him in power probably for several more months, which is enough time to do what is needed to improve his chances before another round of elections. With around more than 90 percent of the votes counted as of late afternoon on Oct. 28, Netanyahu’s Likud party was down 10 Knesset seats from its current parliamentary representation. But the party is still on its way to being the largest faction in the 120-member Knesset by a wide margin with 26 seats, defying the polls and the political fallout from Hamas’s attack on Oct. 7, 2023. The far-right and ultra-Orthodox parties individually had mixed results but, as a group, dropped five seats to 27. The election left the Netanyahu bloc with 53 seats in the Knesset, the biggest by a significant margin—though still short of a minimum 61-seat majority. With the last of the votes due to trickle in only on Friday, Likud may be able to narrow the number of seats it needs—enough for Netanyahu to entertain thoughts of trying to convince lawmakers from the opposition bloc to defect. Yet high-level attendance has fallen precipitously since the first summit in 2019, which drew 43 African heads of state. That number dropped to just 17 in 2023 following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. By comparison, more than 50 attended the last China-Africa summit in 2024. Although Russia has increased engagement across Africa in recent years, its role has largely been limited to a security guarantor, particularly in the Sahel and nations elsewhere dealing with armed conflict, such as Sudan . And low participation in the summit suggests that Russia’s influence across the continent has become increasingly controversial. Notably, the heads of state of the continent’s biggest economies—including South Africa, Nigeria, and Algeria, which is Russia’s biggest African arms buyer—are not attending, though they have sent other officials to lead their delegations, likely in an effort to attract investment. Public backlash against the recruitment of Africans as war fodder has influenced this trend. Other factors include Moscow’s lack of meaningful investment in African countries. They’re trying to do this on a technical question. Is Russia, as it exists today, a legitimate member of the U.N.? A group of Ukrainians, including a former high-ranking diplomat, have cooked up a quixotic plan to strip Russia of one of its most effective political weapons: its platform as a member of the United Nations. They’re trying to do this on a technical question. Is Russia, as it exists today, a legitimate member of the U.N.? Due to the early politics of the United Nations, the Union of Socialist Soviet Republics (USSR) had three seats within the organization—the USSR, the Belarusian SSR and the Ukrainian SSR. The USSR itself was, technically, a federation of many different Soviet republics—including the Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic itself. When the Soviet Union dissolved, its component parts became states—and so, the Ukrainian team argues, the Russian Federation was a new state, not a simple name change from the USSR. States that change their name can be recognized as doing so at the U.N. so simply by sending notification, but the process for recognizing a new state is longer and more complicated. Volodymyr Yelchenko, Ukraine’s former ambassador to the U.N. and an expert member of the Pylyp Orlyk Foundation, a nongovernmental organization named after the man who authored Ukraine’s first constitution in 1710, argued that the acceptance of the newly created Russia as the successor of the USSR was a mistake. “It is the greatest fraud of the century; the Russian Federation never signed the U.N. Charter,” Yelchenko said. Other nations created from the dissolution of larger states have gone through a more elaborate process of formally joining the U.N., But the time, Russia simply wrote an informal letter, rather than undergoing a formal admission vote under Article 4 of the U.N. Charter. “ Bosnia-Herzegovina went through this process, in line with Article 4, in 1992, following the collapse of Yugoslavia,” Yelchenko said. “But the Russians did no such thing; they just changed the nameplate at the table.” The practical chance of the United Nations suspending Russia on a technicality, let alone removing it from its Security Council seat , is extremely s
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/28/ukraine-russia-ussr-united-nations-history/

Each U.S.-stamped deal may make sense on its own terms, and many create huge opportunities for American companies. But as the United States’ partners are increasingly one another’s rivals, this means that Washington is arming and equipping all sides of the region’s many rifts. So do these deals add up to a larger strategy to support the United States’ long-term interests in the region? Not quite. In fact, Washington seems to have no theory of how (or whether) to weigh its many deals against one another. That puts the administration’s own policy goals at risk: Expanding the Abraham Accords, building trade and energy corridors that are insulated from external shocks, and enabling regional partners to carry more of the security burden all depend on these partners not working against one another. The United States does not necessarily need a grand geoeconomic vision for a region that is notoriously unkind to sweeping plans. * How to Not Change Your Mind
  Foreign Policy | 28 Oct 12:02 UTC
  Even when you’re wrong about the Iran war (or anything else).
  FULL TEXT: We are all fallible and life is uncertain, so sometimes we believe things that turn out to be mistaken and we recommend policies that don’t work out as we expected. When this happens, we ought to admit we were wrong, try to figure out why, revise our beliefs accordingly, and then formulate new ideas about what to do in light of our enhanced understanding. In a perfect world, decision-makers, pundits, scholars, and ordinary citizens would be good Bayesians: We’d start with a set of prior beliefs, update them as new evidence accumulates, and adjust our behavior accordingly. In the real world, of course, that happens less often than it should, which is one reason why policies that are visibly failing can be hard to change and why past mistakes keep getting repeated. As historian Barbara Tuchman put it in her book The March of Folly, decision-makers sometimes succumb to a kind of “mental standstill” that “fixes the principles and boundaries governing a political problem.” Both policymakers and pundits are often loath to admit that they made a mistake, even when it is abundantly clear that they were off-base. (For my own attempts to do better, see here and here ). Just look at all those enthusiastic cheerleaders for war with Iran who confidently predicted that it would be quick, cheap, and yield nothing but positive results. How many of them have ‘fessed up and admitted that maybe, just maybe, they missed something ? Instead of owning their mistakes, some of the loudest voices inside and outside the policy world keep insisting that they were right all along. We are all fallible and life is uncertain, so sometimes we believe things that turn out to be mistaken and we recommend policies that don’t work out as we expected. When this happens, we ought to admit we were wrong, try to figure out why, revise our beliefs accordingly, and then formulate new ideas about what to do in light of our enhanced understanding. In a perfect world, decision-makers, pundits, scholars, and ordinary citizens would be good Bayesians: We’d start with a set of prior beliefs, update them as new evidence accumulates, and adjust our behavior accordingly. In the real world, of course, that happens less often than it should, which is one reason why policies that are visibly failing can be hard to change and why past mistakes keep getting repeated. * The Quiet Power of International Orders
  Foreign Policy | 28 Oct 12:00 UTC
  What 2 percent milk reveals about our system of rules and norms.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. When I walk into my neighborhood grocery store and choose to buy a bottle of 2 percent milk, I don’t think about the international order. But I should. My blind trust that I have indeed bought 2 percent milk and not cream or whole milk, which would send my poor stomach into a spin, is a consequence of the order in place today—the liberal international order created by the United States and its allies after World War II. This topic of international order has occupied academics, analysts, and policymakers for the past decade. The arguments made about its unprecedented benefits, such as nonproliferation, global prosperity, and preventing world wars, or the criticisms that it was a myth with liberal ideas selectively applied, are sweeping and grand. But they have little practical meaning for anyone besides elites. Without knowing how the liberal international order, or any international order, is felt in someone’s daily life, it is hard to make the case for either an order’s preservation or its demise. When I walk into my neighborhood grocery store and choose to buy a bottle of 2 percent milk, I don’t think about the international order. But I should. My blind trust that I have indeed bought 2 percent milk and not cream or whole milk, which would send my poor stomach into a spin, is a consequence of the order in place today—the liberal international order created by the United States and its allies after World War II. This topic of international order has occupied academics, analysts, and policymakers for the past decade. The arguments made about its unprecedented benefits, such as nonproliferation, global prosperity, and preventing world wars, or the criticisms that it was a myth with liberal ideas selectively applied, are sweeping and grand. But they have little practical meaning for anyone besides elites. Without knowing how the liberal international order, or any international order, is felt in someone’s daily life, it is hard to make the case for either an order’s preservation or its demise. International order is an encompassing idea of the world—the norms and rules that govern how political entities, countries, empires, and kingdoms do business with each other. But it is also very ordinary. In other words, many of the rules and norms are not splashy; they are quiet, even granular. Let’s take my choice of grocery store milk. In making my choice, I benefit from a norm and rule of the liberal international order. 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/28/international-order-liberal-norms-west-colonial-empire-china/

* Takeaways from Warsh’s news conference.
  NYT Economy | 28 Oct 19:43 UTC
  FULL TEXT: The Federal Reserve held rates steady on Wednesday as expected. The two dissenting officials in favor of a quarter-point increase included Lisa D. Cook, a governor who is fighting to keep her job amid President Trump’s campaign to fire her. Kevin M. Warsh, the Fed’s chairman, leaned into the idea that the central bank had the flexibility to accumulate more information to make the right determination about rates because expectations about future inflation do not point to a lack of confidence in the Fed’s ability to eventually achieve the 2 percent target and because there are no signs the economy is overheating. Mr. Warsh’s biggest announcement of the day pertained to how often the Fed will vote on interest rates in the coming years, an idea that The New York Times first reported the chairman raised in July. In 2027, the Fed plans to hold seven policy meetings, with one dedicated to discussing “broad monetary policy strategy.” In 2028, there will be six policy meetings and two on strategy. Mr. Warsh repeatedly mentioned a shift in financial conditions since September, which he suggested helped the Fed in the pursuit of its inflation goal on the margins. Credit conditions have “tightened for some businesses and households, much as we would expect,” he said. “Market participants are becoming somewhat more discerning in their capital allocations, and while overall financial conditions are tighter, I’m unwilling and unable to call them tight. But broadly, these developments go in the direction of achieving a longer, more durable period of economic growth and price stability.” The Fed’s meeting is days before the midterm elections, but Mr. Warsh emphasized that politics played no role in Wednesday’s decision to hold rates steady. But the political backdrop has been hard to ignore, especially because of Mr. Trump’s repeated attacks on the Fed for not complying with his demands for lower rates. The White House has demanded that Ms. Cook attend an in-person hearing to defend herself against allegations that she committed mortgage fraud, something Mr. Trump has cited as a reason to fire her. The S&P 500 ended the day 0.3 percent higher, after a volatile period of trading while Warsh was speaking. Easier monetary policy, with rates expectations falling, is usually bullish for the stock market. Starting next year, the Federal Reserve will reduce the number of meetings at which it sets interest rates, Kevin M. Warsh, the central bank’s chairman, announced Wednesday. Th
  https://www.nytimes.com/live/2026/10/28/business/fed-meeting-warsh-interest-rates/takeaways-from-warshs-news-conference

* UK budget 2026: Key climate and energy announcements
  Carbon Brief | 28 Oct 18:16 UTC [also carried by: BBC Business]

  FULL TEXT: Chancellor John Healey has announced a “new course on energy” for the UK, focused on keeping bills low amid the global crisis triggered by war in the Middle East. As he laid out his first budget, Healey said the Labour government would still prioritise the expansion of clean power and remain “unshakably committed to net-zero”. The budget sets out what the chancellor calls a “radical reform of energy”, including a longer-term effort to “break the grip of gas on bills”. There are also a variety of short-term measures to curb energy bills, including a council-tax rebate and the removal of the gas “standing charge”, though these fell far short of pre-budget calls to tackle high prices. Moreover, in order to fund defence spending, the government is cutting £1bn from its “ warm homes plan ” and £700m from hydrogen and carbon capture and storage (CCS) projects. The budget also appears to cut up to another £4.7bn from the warm homes plan – as well as ending zero-rated VAT for solar panels and extending a “temporary” 5p cut in fuel duty. Finally, the government set out plans to ensure new data centres meet strict environmental regulations, while leaving the door open to on-site fossil-fuel power generation. Below, Carbon Brief runs through the key climate- and energy-focused announcements from the budget. Conflict in the Middle East has driven up energy bills for people in the UK, as the global trade in oil and gas has been thrown into disarray. In this context, the new budget sets out a “shift in the government’s approach to energy”, focusing on “cheaper energy” as the main priority. At the same time, Healey was clear that fossil fuels were driving the price increases and that clean power would be essential for the government’s cost-cutting agenda. In its accompanying analysis , the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) notes that “fossil-fuel prices rose by 83% in the year to September 2026”. In his speech, the chancellor announced a “radical reform of energy to reduce bills”, stating: “Today, I’m setting a new course on energy. To get UK energy costs down in line with Europe, from this point, our Labour government priority is first and foremost cheaper energy, with clean power key to achieving this. This is possible because of the progress we have made towards clean power over the last two years.” Conservative and Reform politicians have argued that the UK should abandon its net-zero targets, including Labour’s “clean power by 2030” goal, in order to cut energy

* Revealed: UK relied on £2bn of ‘questionable accounting’ to hit its climate-aid goal
  Carbon Brief | 28 Oct 07:00 UTC
  The UK has beaten its target to provide £11.6bn of climate aid to developing countries by 2026 – but only… The post Revealed: UK relied on £2bn of ‘questionable accounting’ to hit its climate-aid goal appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The UK has beaten its target to provide £11.6bn of climate aid to developing countries by 2026 – but only through “questionable accounting”. As part of its obligations under the Paris Agreement , the UK pledged to give at least £11.6bn in overseas “ climate finance ” between 2021 and 2026. Now, Carbon Brief analysis reveals that around £2.1bn of the funds the UK counted towards this goal did not come from new spending. Instead, the government relabelled existing funding for development banks, private-sector investment and aid for Afghanistan, Sudan and other war-torn nations as “climate finance”. Without these changes, the UK would have fallen nearly £1bn short of its target. Carbon Brief’s analysis is based on numerous FOI requests and reveals the otherwise-unpublished details of the UK’s climate-finance spending. Experts and MPs say the use of “questionable” accounting shows the UK is “cutting corners” to hit its climate goals, while making unprecedented cuts to its foreign aid budget. The UK and other developed countries are obliged under UN climate treaties to provide “ new and additional ” climate finance to help developing countries tackle climate change. They also have specific targets to “mobilise” $100bn of climate finance per year through to 2025 and “at least” $300bn by 2035. As the UK’s contribution to international climate finance, in 2019 the Conservative government pledged to provide £11.6bn between the financial years 2021-22 and 2025-26. This amounted to “ doubling ” its previous five-year target. Since then, successive governments have slashed the amount of money going towards foreign aid, citing economic strain and a need for more defence spending. This has placed great pressure on the £11.6bn target, which comes from the UK aid budget. In order to retain the target while cutting aid, the Conservative government decided in 2023 to loosen the criteria for what it defined as “climate” spending. This enabled the UK to remain committed to the £11.6bn goal, without spending more on new climate projects, by simply relabelling existing funds. The current Labour government has opted to maintain this approach, while pledging to cut aid spending even further . The move has been strongly criticised by campaigners , MPs and even the UK’s official aid watchdog , who have described it as “ creative accounting ” and “ moving the goalposts ”. The UK government has announced that it met the £11.6bn target, but has not released the full details of how it 
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/revealed-uk-relied-on-2bn-of-questionable-accounting-to-hit-its-climate-aid-goal

* Analysis: How the UK spent more than £11.6bn on climate aid since 2021
  Carbon Brief | 28 Oct 07:00 UTC
  The UK has spent £12.8bn on climate aid for developing countries since 2021, according to official figures obtained by Carbon… The post Analysis: How the UK spent more than £11.6bn on climate aid since 2021 appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The UK has spent £12.8bn on climate aid for developing countries since 2021, according to official figures obtained by Carbon Brief. Freedom-of-information (FOI) requests reveal how the UK has used its “ climate finance ” to support everything from solar projects in Mozambique to carbon offsets in the Amazon . UK climate funding, which reached a record £4bn this year, has supported roughly 500 aid projects, climate funds and private-investment initiatives over the past five years. With this spending, the UK has surpassed its £11.6bn climate-finance target for this period, although Carbon Brief analysis shows that it has partly relied on “ questionable accounting ”. This target is part of the UK’s ongoing responsibility under UN climate treaties to financially support developing countries in their efforts to tackle climate change. Despite being central to its international climate policy, the UK government does not routinely release data on progress towards its climate-finance goals. Here, Carbon Brief has published the data in full for the first time and pulled out key trends since 2021. According to the FOI data obtained by Carbon Brief, at least 493 projects, funds and institutions received UK climate finance between 2021-22 and 2025-26. These included £45m to help Ukraine curb its reliance on fossil fuels, £40m in cash transfers for Kenyans affected by drought and £30m to provide flood resilience in Nepal. Larger sums were provided to projects spread across multiple countries, often with the stated goal of “mobilising” additional finance from the private sector. For example, the UK channelled £198m into a World Bank-backed project to help Colombia, Cambodia and others create and sell “high-integrity” carbon offsets. The UK also provided £229m to a group that uses foreign aid to attract private capital for projects in predominantly low-income nations, such as a solar farm in Uganda and a “ sustainable aviation fuel ” project in Pakistan. All of the funded projects can be viewed in the interactive table below. The UK has been criticised by campaigners and MPs for its lack of transparency around climate finance data. In line with its international obligations , the government routinely releases its climate-finance figures to the UN – albeit with a two-year delay. However, it does not publish this data broken down by financial year, making it impossible to accurately gauge progress towards the £11.6bn goal. The data gathered by Carbon Brief is therefore the
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-how-the-uk-spent-more-than-11-6bn-on-climate-aid-since-2021

* JPMorgan, Coca-Cola Among 7 More Companies Objecting to OKX-ICE Tokenized Stocks
  Decrypt | 28 Oct 15:05 UTC
  Seven more companies, including JPMorgan Chase, Coca-Cola and Dell, have objected to trading of their shares on the planned OKX-ICE tokenized stock venue, bringing the total to 33.
  FULL TEXT: The pushback against OKX and ICE’s tokenized stock venue now includes the biggest bank in the U.S. JPMorgan Chase, Coca-Cola, and Dell Technologies are among seven companies added to OKXICE TSV’s list of objecting issuers in a notice dated Tuesday . Advanced Micro Devices, Adobe, Applied Materials, and Sandisk round out the group. The additions bring the total to 33, up from 26 a day earlier. The list has grown quickly. It stood at nine on Oct. 15, 16 on Oct. 19 , and 22 on Oct. 23 . Earlier objectors include Microsoft, Alphabet, Goldman Sachs, Wells Fargo, Intel, and Oracle. JPMorgan is the third major bank to object, after Goldman and Wells Fargo. Under the SEC’s innovation exemption , which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue. The notices haven’t disclosed the companies’ reasons. OKXICE’s original Oct. 4 notice proposed 63 tokenized stocks from 62 companies, and several objectors, including Wells Fargo and Arm, weren’t on that initial roster. Some of the biggest names, including Nvidia, Tesla, Apple, and Amazon, haven’t objected so far. OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7. Trades would run through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC. Earlier notices warned that it’s uncertain whether tokenized shares in self-custodied wallets would be covered by the Securities Investor Protection Corporation if the tokenizer’s broker-dealer failed. OKX and ICE first teamed up on tokenized securities earlier this year before announcing the 24/7 venue this month. Nasdaq, which won SEC approval for its own tokenized securities pilot, has said it will opt its shares out of the exemption. Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus original features, a podcast, videos and more.
  https://decrypt.co/382043/jpmorgan-coca-cola-7-more-companies-objecting-okx-ice-tokenized-stocks

Without the Arab parties, the Zionist opposition parties cannot form a government. On the horizon looms the “Square Colosseum,” Mussolini’s monument to fascist grandeur, a reminder of Italy’s architectural integration of this part of its past rather than its total erasure. The sheep, it seems, pay it no mind: They have grazed and resisted change in this land for centuries, in the least expected places. In the Italian capital, sheep have always been part of the metropolitan landscape. It’s estimated there are roughly 50,000 of them spread all over the city. In Monte Cucco, Antonio’s 200 or so animals can be seen grazing. Later, after these interviews but before the time of writing, the flock will be removed to make way for a so-called requalification project that doesn’t envision their presence: a modern park. Most of the sheep will be sent to slaughterhouses. Others will be sold. And as a result, because there are no sheep to tame it, the grass on the hill of Monte Cucco will grow tall, reaching more than 6 feet high, and fires will break out. Rome (and, by extension, perhaps other cities in Italy) has to contend with a fundamental question: What to do with such ancient traditions? Some residents can picture coexistence with the animals, while others fear that “children will get too close to the droppings,” as one resident says. Chiara Visentin, scientific director of the Emilio Sereni Library-Archive at the Alcide Cervi Institute, explains that the legacy of the Enlightenment period can be seen today in people’s repulsion at living alongside the animals, which was once considered normal but is now automatically associated with unpleasant smells, disease and inconvenience. And although the tradition is challenged in Rome, it remains visible. It’s just a waste of time,” he says while he points to his sheep. Each one has a name: Bianchina, Carolina, Graziella. “I put bells on all of them because I like the sound. * The Amarna letters
  Aeon | 28 Oct 10:01 UTC
  Petty disputes, pleas for help and requests for gold – explore letters sent to Egyptian pharaohs more than 3,000 years ago - by Aeon Video Watch on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: First discovered in Egypt in the late 19th century, the collection of clay tablets known as the Amarna letters is one of our richest windows into the ancient world. The letters are extraordinary not only for their contents – diplomatic communications exchanged between Egyptian pharaohs and other nearby rulers in the 14th century BCE – but also for their script: they were written not in Egyptian hieroglyphs but largely in cuneiform, a writing system most often associated with ancient Mesopotamia. In this video from the British Museum, the always entertaining Assyriologist Irving Finkel details how the letters came to be and how they were discovered, and delves into the fascinating correspondence itself, which includes petty disputes over perceived slights, leaders lobbying for military support and more than a few requests for gold.   The would-be democrats of Serbia’s Student List find themselves in a complex situation: Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. The post Serbia’s Victorious New Democrats Face an Old Question appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: For the second time in just over a quarter-century, Serbia has seemingly thrown off the yoke of one-man rule. In October 2000, Serbians mobilized — first at the ballot box, then in the streets — to oust the genocidal regime of Slobodan Milošević . In October 2026, it was sufficient for them merely to vote in overwhelming numbers to force the concession of the strongman at the helm. This time Serbians ousted Milošević’s protege, Aleksandar Vučić, and his ruling Serbian Progressive Party (SNS) after their nearly 14-year tenure in government. The vote capped two years of protest that began in November 2024, when a newly renovated canopy at Novi Sad’s railway station collapsed, killing 16 people. An insurgent student movement blamed the deaths on government corruption, which they saw as a hallmark of Vučić’s rule, and their ensuing blockades of universities grew into the largest protest movement in Serbia since 2000. The movement eventually coalesced into the so-called Student List parliamentary coalition. On Sunday, this bloc won 53% of the popular vote and secured around 140 seats in the country’s 250-seat National Assembly, providing it with a comfortable, stable governing majority. Domestically, the Student List ran on a sweeping anti-corruption platform. Its foreign policy agenda, however, is virtually indistinguishable from that of the SNS — at least on paper. Why? Because Serbian nationalism remains, arguably, the most potent political force in the country. The vote that propelled the Serbian List into power is a heterodox amalgam of the country’s moderate, liberal public and the antiregime far right (voters who believe Vučić has been insufficiently hard-line, for instance, in opposing Kosovo’s sovereignty, and would like the country to pursue still deeper ties with Moscow). The same curious combination was evident throughout the two-year protest movement preceding the vote. A very similar coalition toppled Milošević in 2000. This is the bind in which Serbia’s would-be democrats find themselves. Their aspirations to remake the country’s political order depend, in part, on the support of some of the region’s most reactionary elements. Yet the past 40 years suggest that, much as in Russia, Serbia’s regional ambitions and democratization at home are at odds. The reason is institutional. The Banville is short and enjoyable but has been a victim of the smorgasbord approach, never quite seizing priority. Also on my Libby shelf are, among others, Lovers XXX by Allie Rowbottom, The Original by Nell Stevens, and Villa Coco by Andrew Sean Greer. * Love with nowhere to go
  Aeon | 27 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Grief is not only for the dead. When we are unable to honour other profound losses, we become strangers to ourselves - by Derek van Zoonen Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Telemark, Norway, 2011. Photo by Jonas Bendiksen/Magnum Photos I have been grieving my mother for years, even though she is still alive. Sometimes before falling asleep – in that liminal space between waking and sleeping where the self becomes porous – I’d see the image of the sweet and caring woman who would come to say goodnight, her cheeks covered in thick layers of night cream. Then, out of nowhere, the image would flip into the raging woman who was so entangled in her own suffering that she turned her frustration on us. Over the years, that second woman slowly swallowed the first, as she got pulled into my brother’s addiction. It’s hard to say where her own unhappiness ended and my brother’s suffering began, but together they drifted into a parallel reality, cut off from the world beyond their heads. There was no funeral for this. No condolence cards. No flowers. No phone calls. No leave of absence. What was I supposed to mourn? And yet what I felt for them, what I still feel, has the unmistakable gravity of mourning. Can there be grief without death? I have come to think that we grieve much more often than we realise, and that death is grief’s most visible occasion rather than its essence. If that doesn’t match your experience, it may be because we rarely call these other losses by their name. Most of our losses arrive without a funeral. That is the human predicament: we are creatures built to attach, living in a world where nothing stays. Sooner or later, we lose the things we care about. And since we are complex beings with a body, a mind, a community and an environment – beings who love many different things – there is a great deal we can lose, from our physical health to our sense of who we are, from relationships to homelands to the natural world we share with other species. For creatures like us, there is no way around heartbreak: to live is to lose. Grief is what love does when its object is gone. It is love with nowhere to go. Over the past few years I have accumulated many such losses. There is the psychological loss of my father as I knew and loved him: a brutal stroke rendered him incapable of walking and cycling, and cycling was the only thing that could ‘make his thoughts liquid’, as he used to say. There is the loss of old friendships. First you are no longer part of each other’s lives – you just update one another about your life – and then, one day, it becomes awkward to reach out at all. * AI models cost more when you don’t use English
  Rest of World | 26 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Some languages require far more tokens for AI models to process, raising costs and reducing context for the people who use them.
  FULL TEXT: People who use less widely spoken languages could end up paying more to use artificial intelligence than those who speak English or other dominant languages. A new study published last month by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found AI models require far more tokens for some languages than others. Tokens are the units of text or data that the models process. The researchers have called this a hidden “tokenizer tax,” which can make AI more expensive in languages with smaller online presence. A tokenizer is a tool that breaks down raw text, images, or audio into tokens. Even within the 20 most popular languages on the internet, there is a gap in token usage, the research found. The most expensive of the languages used between 1.5 and 5 times more tokens than the cheapest. This gap widened when researchers expanded the set to 200 languages, with the token cost gap in one AI model growing by 17 times. “A startup in [Bengaluru] may pay a surcharge on every prompt and completion while its competitor in Austin does not,” the study said. “The tokenizer tax also has implications beyond cost. Higher token counts translate into higher latency and reduced effective context capacity. These penalties hit hardest where compute and connectivity are already limited. Tokenizer inequity is a constraint on who can participate in the AI economy.” Non-English languages leave less room for documents or conversation history, and require more work to generate the answer, according to Pratyush Choudhury, co-founder of Activate AI, India’s first venture capital fund dedicated exclusively to AI. Activate backs multilingual voice AI startups like ElevenLabs, Sarvam, and Wispr Flow. “Think about a tutor: Retaining the student’s earlier mistakes and explanations is part of delivering a good lesson. If that history consumes the context window much faster in one language, the disadvantage reaches into the quality of the service,” he said. One way to improve language processing is to improve the underlying data. “Open data efforts matter here, like RESPIN, a collaborative initiative with [the Indian Institute of Science] funded by the Gates Foundation, where we collected text from native speakers of 38 dialects along with read speech,” Raoul Nanavati, co-founder and CEO of Mumbai-based AI voice infrastructure startup Navana.ai , told Rest of World . However, the study by the Coalition for Language Equity in AI Research found the rarity of a language isn’t the only determina
  https://restofworld.org/2026/ai-tokenizer-tax-non-english-languages-cost-inequity/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

I was a doctoral student at the time, researching economic autonomy in Kurdistan, and it became clear that this work would no longer be tenable in the new political climate. I moved to Latin America, to complete my doctoral studies and study revolutionary movements in that part of the world, grappling all the while with a question that had become inseparable from my own existence: how could peace be built in Kurdistan?   New Lines | 23 Oct 15:19 UTC
  Palestinian citizens of Israel face considerable discrimination, with racist rhetoric and marginalization increasing since Oct. 7. Nevertheless, and despite considerable evidence to the contrary, some are convinced that the system can be changed from within. The post ‘You’re Not Supposed To Be Here’ appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: One of the loudest, most vocal — and most surprising — advocates of Israeli policy to emerge since the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks and the start of the Gaza war is a Palestinian journalist who says he is “a proud Zionist.” Haifa-born Yousef Haddad joined the Israeli army after high school and served in a combat unit (Palestinian citizens are exempt from mandatory conscription but may volunteer to serve), then went on to a career as a peripatetic Israel advocate. He has debated on behalf of Israel at the Oxford Union, promoted Israeli policy in Arabic on the Saudi-owned Al Arabiya, visited campuses all over the U.S. and is a regular guest commentator on Israeli television. Now, just ahead of the national election on Oct. 27, he has entered politics as a candidate for a new far-right party called Amcha Yisrael (Your Nation Israel). It is headed by a retired brigadier general, Ofer Winter, a religious Zionist and father of eight who advocates “voluntary emigration” for the Palestinians of Gaza. And yet, for all his pro-Israel posturing, Haddad has not earned the affection of anyone. Israeli Jews who oppose Netanyahu see him as a shill, and Palestinian citizens see him as a native informant. The center-right Jewish majority is happy to celebrate him opportunistically for his propaganda value, but they are suspicious of all Arabs, especially since Oct. 7, so they have declined to embrace him. Religious nationalist Zionists reject him because they are ideologically committed to an ethnically pure Jewish state. Haddad, however, is undeterred; if anything, recent attacks from pro-Netanyahu detractors who have implied he is a fifth columnist seem to have energized him. He continues to deliver his far-right talking points in an increasingly aggressive, bombastic style. * A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes
  New Lines | 23 Oct 10:24 UTC
  A hundred years after its founding, the elite, secular Laique school in Damascus has decided to separate boys and girls for the first time. Syrians are asking what that means for the school, and the future of Syria. The post A Change at a Damascus School Has Syrians Reading the Runes appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Exactly 100 years ago, a secular French school opened in Damascus with 79 students, in rented premises close to where the current parliament stands on Abid Street. The school was known then as the Laique Institute and is known today as the Al-Hurriya Institute, but is generally referred to as just Laique. Four years later, in 1930, then-Prime Minister Taj al-Din al-Hasani, a turbaned cleric with a reformist agenda, granted the school a plot of land on Baghdad Street, to which it formally relocated in 1932. Laique quickly became one of the most prestigious institutions in Syria, celebrated for its coeducation, the quality of its faculty, and its tennis, volleyball and basketball courts. It also had an impressive library, along with a full-fledged theater that once hosted Egyptian diva Umm Kulthum in 1955. That colorful tapestry was part of a Middle East that imagined itself as part of a broader Arab world with a united identity. Fast forward 100 years, and Laique has become a shadow of its former self. First came 61 years of Baath Party rule that imposed an indoctrinating socialist curriculum on the entire Syrian school system, along with khaki uniforms. Teachers at Laique were hired not because they were good, but because they were Baathists. Then came the 1967 nationalization that ended the school’s autonomy and distorted its institutional identity, renaming it “Mahad al-Huriyya,” or the “Institute of Freedom,” a nod to the language of Baathism, with its three ideological pillars of freedom, pan-Arabism and socialism. The fact that Hafez al-Assad chose Laique for all his children — Bashar included — only hardened the school’s Baathism. When Assad’s eldest son and chosen successor, Bassel al-Assad, died in 1994, the school was renamed after him. What did not disappear was the memory of what Laique had been, and, more importantly, its alumni network of engineers, doctors, politicians and artists who became the “who’s who” of Syrian society. For decades, “I went to Laique” functioned as a kind of passport, akin to “I went to Brummana High School” in Lebanon: a signal of academic excellence that guaranteed entry into the workforce and came with high expectations of talent and character. A great deal of Syrian history was compressed into a single address. This was a school founded under the French mandate, legitimized by a reformist cleric premier, staffed by French and Syrian teachers, and attended by the children of the urban bourgeoisie. * Traction!
  Aeon | 23 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Tribologists study friction: what happens when things rub together. It’s indispensable, from the wheel to the paths of stars - by Jennifer R Vail Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: In conversations, few words catch my attention faster than ‘friction’ – and especially talk of making things frictionless . From the workplace to grocery shopping, our societies seem to be striving to remove friction from everything. The motivation appears to be that decreasing resistance makes life easier and smoother. Having spent a career as a tribologist, studying friction, wear and lubrication scientifically, I have some opinions on this. I’ll confess that, in most of my career, I too have sought to reduce friction, developing new materials, designs or even instrumentation in order to lower friction in a jet engine or reduce emissions from your car. However, I’ve also found myself trying to figure out how to increase friction to remove plaque on teeth. The reality is that friction, despite seeming so antagonistic, is necessary and even productive. The force that resists motion has, since humans first struck a spark from a stone, been the very thing that propels us forward. And this paradox offers a lens through which to reread humanity’s entire technological history. I n order to appreciate all that an understanding of friction has helped us to accomplish, it’s necessary to start at square one and state what friction actually is. We define friction as the force that resists motion, and that means friction is rather ubiquitous. If things are moving, whether they’re sliding around or rolling, there’s friction. Solids, liquids, gases? Doesn’t matter. If there’s movement, friction is there to resist it. Unfortunately, because friction’s job is to resist, we’ve developed an aversion to it as a nuisance. But it’s a very important force, and we’ve thrived by expanding our understanding of it. By the 1960s, after millennia of evolving with friction, we finally accepted that friction is such a critical part of our world that a scientific field of study needed to be devoted to it: tribology. Tribologists like me study this force in all its forms. Tribology is the science of interacting surfaces in relative motion, concerned with the wear of materials, friction and lubrication. Basically, tribology is the specialisation of things rubbing against each other. You may raise an eyebrow at a field of science focused on this, but when you pause to consider how much motion is going on inside us, around us and throughout the Universe, you can appreciate that tribology is vast. More than anyone, tribologists know the problems friction can cause. But they also know the ac
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-a-world-without-friction-is-a-world-without-progress

We Saudis find ourselves in a unique position, different from that of the other parties involved. We have no voice that represents us under these circumstances, despite being directly affected by the war. I spoke with families, academics and others in the kingdom in recent days to inquire about what was happening on the ground and how it was being received by the population at large. But that sense of stability has now faded, and concerns over domestic issues have intensified with the war, despite the crown prince’s reforms. Another told me that “we’ve gotten used to hearing Houthi missiles.” Perhaps those were not missiles but the sounds of drones. I don’t know, and I don’t think the person I spoke to cares about the difference. The young children in this person’s family are excited because school has been suspended, but that does not change the fact that there is a war going on, and that hearing drones and explosions has become normalized for many Saudis. What is even more troubling is that excessive nationalism has taken hold. Although Saudi Arabia is home to a large population of foreign workers and residents, whose numbers are close to those of Saudi citizens, the current crisis has also brought unfair treatment toward some foreigners. Many Saudis are demanding that foreigners, most of whom are in the country simply to earn a living, publicly express their opinions and show support for the kingdom. Today the town has 20,000 residents, but in its 14th-century heyday it boasted almost four times as many souls. The late Middle Ages was a tough time to be alive, between the Black Death, various crusades and holy wars, and famine—not to mention the labor conditions of silver mining. Kutná Hora was also a popular place to die, and all the villagers wanted to spend eternity at the medieval Sedlec Abbey. After one of the crusades, an abbot had brought back a handful of soil, supposedly from Golgotha, and strewn it on the abbey grounds. Burial in that soil was believed to grant quicker access to heaven, which led to an overcrowded cemetery. The solution? Build a subterranean chapel to house the remains. And then a half-blind monk, in the wake of a terrible plague, exhumed the victims and used them to redecorate. An ossuary is any repository for human remains, but it’s also the term for a holy site that serves this function. Another name for this kind of church is charnel house, which to me evokes the image of an abattoir. Death historian Philippe Ariès connects the term to the French idiom une vieille carne , translated as an old nag or a tough piece of meat.   This photograph taken on September 6, 2026 shows a placard opposing data centers displayed on a water dispenser during the People's Convention on AI Data Centers event at Visakhapatnam in India's Andhra Pradesh state.   FULL TEXT: After ChatGPT was released in November 2022, the tech industry was quick to herald the chatbot as the marker of a new wave of technological progress. As companies pushed to create more powerful AI models, the water demand of their ambitions took a while to become clear. In April 2023, a research paper out of the University of California estimated that training the large language model behind the initial version of ChatGPT had required 185,000 gallons of water. For every extended conversation someone had with the chatbot or every time they asked it to write an email, they were effectively pouring a bottle of water down the drain. That statistic got a lot of play when it was released. It became an early warning sign of what was coming as companies pushed chatbots as a universal technology that could replace psychologists, educators , lawyers, and even people’s friends or partners . As public concern grew, Google and OpenAI came out with their own figures. The companies suggested that querying one of their chatbots used mere drops of water. Independent researchers were not impressed, arguing the companies were taking a narrow view of the water use necessary to power and cool increasingly monstrous data centers. Those water demands can be quite significant too. A hyperscale data center can suck up between 1 and 5 million gallons of water a day, comparable to the amount used by a city of 10,000 to 50,000 people — depending on the part of the world we’re talking about. Consider how these companies often cluster data centers together and it becomes obvious what a challenge it can be to supply them. Data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. The water demands of individual data centers are also continuing to grow. It’s not just that they need more water for cooling as they become larger and contain more servers. The graphics processing units (GPUs) that power generative AI are much more resource-intensive than the chips and storage devices that traditionally filled data center racks. GPUs run harder and throw off more heat , meaning they also need more energy or water to keep them cool. Data centers cannot just use any kind of water either; it has to be clean enough to ensure the equipment and pipes are not damaged. Tapping into public drinking water is both easier and cheaper than paying the cost of treatment themselves. That means data centers end up competing with communities over their potable water supply. * Other people
  Aeon | 22 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Disconnection is at the heart of today’s crises. So my husband and I chose a radical and revealing remedy: living communally - by Elizabeth Oldfield Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Earlier this year, I surfaced from sleep to a strange noise, a loud, human singing reverberating through our bedroom ceiling, then a low, almost animal groaning. My husband and I looked at each other. ‘I think Hatty’s in labour,’ he said. The rest of the day proceeded in the usual way, getting breakfast for our children, dropping them off at school, meetings, emails, booking in the car for an MOT, but these activities were interspersed with opening the door to midwives, making cups of tea for Hatty, and finding towels to drape around the birth pool that had appeared in our living room. Towards evening, our eighth housemate appeared in a rush of fluids. Two ambulance-loads of paramedics stood around like a flock of green and yellow London parakeets, blessedly unrequired. At one point, there were nine medical professionals, seven housemates, two grandparents of the new arrival and a cat in our terraced London house. It didn’t even feel that full, I thought later, as I poured bleach on bloodstained towels and binned a discarded catheter that had been overlooked in a corner. I am 42, and I did not expect to end up here. Like most of my peers, I was handed a vision of a home worth striving for with far, far fewer people in it and, to begin with, I followed that script. A middle-class 1980s child, I did well at school, moved away from my home county dormitory town for university, and never went back. London, jobs at the BBC, running a think tank all followed. I lived with good friends, and juggled seeing others between our clashing personal schedules. Then people began to pair up and procreate. I met someone, followed suit, and we clawed our way onto the very bottom of the housing ladder. It was a lovely life. A bit relentless, perhaps, a bit lonely around the edges, too, as the schedules got ever harder to tessellate, the work and care burdens heavier. But we had done it. Our little dyad, with dependants, was standing on its own feet, even if something felt decidedly unstable underneath. More space, more privacy, more control – all are sold as natural, unquestioned goods to be sought in the unconscious script that most of us carry. For the nuclear family, they are tokens of success and maturity, whereas forgoing these ‘goods’ and living with people you are neither shagging nor parenting has an association with low-status, studenty seasons.
